People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Yeah, um... well, to be honest, my manager at the restaurant is the one who said I really needed to come in and talk to somebody. I'm a waitress, and usually, you know, I'm really good at my job. I mean, I went to college for a little while, so I know how to manage my time and multitask, which you absolutely have to do in the service industry because the dinner rush just gets completely insane. You have to keep a million things straight in your head at once. 

But lately... I don't know, things have just been getting really, really mixed up. And, well, what really scared everyone at work—and honestly, it kind of confused me too—is that I started just... freezing up. Right in the middle of a busy service. Like, the dining room is packed, people are waiting for their drinks or their food, and I'd just completely stop moving. Or I'd start doing things totally out of order. Like, this one shift recently, I was supposed to be cashing people out, closing their checks, right? But instead, I somehow ended up just standing over by the soda station. I was just standing there by the ice machine, holding the key to the cash drawer tight in my hand, and I just kept repeating table numbers out loud over and over. Like, 'table four, table twelve, table nine...' just saying them, instead of actually walking over to the register and closing the checks. My manager had to come over and ask what I was doing, and it just really freaked everybody out because I guess it looked really strange, but I just couldn't get the steps right in my head to do what I was supposed to be doing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I didn't black out at all. That is actually the most frustrating part of the whole thing, to be perfectly honest with you. If I had just fainted or lost time, maybe it would make more sense to everybody, but I was completely awake and aware of every single second. I remember exactly what was happening. I remember the exact sound the ice machine was making—it was doing that loud grinding noise it does when it is refilling the bin, which it always does right around eight o'clock. And I could hear the ticket machine just going absolutely crazy back in the kitchen, just printing and printing. 

I could see my manager, Greg, walking toward me, and I even remember he was wearing his blue striped tie, and I could see the exact look on his face, this really specific mix of annoyed and confused. I was totally there. I could feel the cold metal of the cash drawer key digging right into the palm of my hand because I was squeezing it so hard. I just... I couldn't make my legs walk back to the register. I knew in my head that I needed to go over there, I kept telling myself to just walk to the screen and swipe my card and close out the tables, but instead I just stood there. I was smelling the fried calamari coming from the expo window, and listening to the dining room chatter, and the table numbers just kept spilling out of my mouth. 

So, no, definitely no lost time. I almost wish I had blacked out, because being fully conscious while you are just stuck like that, with everyone staring at you... it is really humiliating. I remember every single detail, unfortunately.

I mean, I went to college for a little while, so I know how to manage my time and multitask, which you absolutely have to do in the service industry because the dinner rush just gets completely insane. You have to keep a million things straight in your head at once. \n\nBut lately... I don't know, things have just been getting really, really mixed up. I was just standing there by the ice machine, holding the key to the cash drawer tight in my hand, and I just kept repeating table numbers out loud over and over. Like, 'table four, table twelve, table nine...' just saying them, instead of actually walking over to the register and closing the checks. I couldn't make my legs walk back to the register. Well, see, that's the thing about time when something like that happens, it gets really tricky to pin down exactly. Because when you're just standing there, and the ice machine is grinding, and the ticket printer is going off, seconds can feel like absolutely forever. I mean, if I had to guess, maybe it was just a few minutes? But it felt like I was standing there for an hour, just completely locked in place. 

I remember Greg, my manager, he came over and he was talking to me, and usually he's very quick, very impatient, you know how restaurant managers are, they always have a million fires to put out. So the fact that he actually stopped and stood there with me means it must have been long enough for him to realize something was really wrong, not just me taking a quick breather. And then he kind of guided me to the back office, which is right past the walk-in cooler, and I sat in his chair—it's this really squeaky black leather chair—and I just sat there for I don't even know how long before I finally felt like I could actually move my arms and legs properly again. 

So, I don't know exactly how long the whole thing lasted from start to finish. I really couldn't give you an exact number of minutes. I just know it was long enough to completely ruin the dinner service and make everyone think I was losing my mind, which, honestly, is how it felt to me, too. It's just so hard to judge time when you're trapped in your own body like that, you know?

Um... well. I guess... yeah. Actually, yes. And I honestly wasn't sure if I should even mention this because it just sounds so, I don't know, ridiculous when you say it out loud, but since you're asking... yes. 

It's mostly when I'm doing the closing shift. And while I'm standing there counting, I will hear... well, I'll hear this woman's voice. 

And it's not like a faint whisper or something you could mistake for the wind outside or the hum of the big refrigerators in the back. It is loud and clear, like she is standing right over my shoulder. Sometimes she just says my name, really clearly, or... or she'll actually tell me I'm doing the count wrong. She'll say something like, 'you miscounted that,' or 'that's not the right total.' And it sounds so incredibly real, so completely normal, that my first instinct is just to respond like I would to anyone else. So I'll literally say out loud, 'No, I didn't,' or 'What are you talking about?' And then I turn around, fully expecting to see one of the other servers who maybe forgot something, or my manager, and there is absolutely no one there. The room is completely empty.

I close a lot on Tuesdays and Thursdays because I really need the extra hours, and by the end of the night, the dining room is totally empty, all the chairs are flipped upside down on the tables, and the music is off. It's usually just me out front rolling the last of the silverware or doing the final count on my drawer. You have to make sure all your cash and credit card slips match up perfectly before you can leave, which takes a lot of focus, especially after an eight-hour shift on your feet. And while I'm standing there counting, I will hear... well, I'll hear this woman's voice. \n\nAnd it's not like a faint whisper or something you could mistake for the wind outside or the hum of the big refrigerators in the back. And it sounds so incredibly real, so completely normal, that my first instinct is just to respond like I would to anyone else. You have to keep a million things straight in your head at once. \n\nBut lately... I don't know, things have just been getting really, really mixed up. And, well, what really scared everyone at work—and honestly, it kind of confused me too—is that I started just... freezing up. Right in the middle of a busy service. Like, the dining room is packed, people are waiting for their drinks or their food, and I'd just completely stop moving. Or I'd start doing things totally out of order. Like, this one shift recently, I was supposed to be cashing people out, closing their checks, right? But instead, I somehow ended up just standing over by the soda station. I was just standing there by the ice machine, holding the key to the cash drawer tight in my hand, and I just kept repeating table numbers out loud over and over. Like, 'table four, table twelve, table nine...' just saying them, instead of actually walking over to the register and closing the checks. My manager had to come over and ask what I was doing, and it just really freaked everybody out because I guess it looked really strange, but I just couldn't get the steps right in my head to do what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: During that episode when you were frozen by the soda station repeating table numbers, were you aware of what was happening around you and able to remember it afterward, or did you feel like you 'blacked out' and lost time?\n\nPatient: No, no, I didn't black out at all. That is actually the most frustrating part of the whole thing, to be perfectly honest with you. If I had just fainted or lost time, maybe it would make more sense to everybody, but I was completely awake and aware of every single second. I remember exactly what was happening. I remember the exact sound the ice machine was making—it was doing that loud grinding noise it does when it is refilling the bin, which it always does right around eight o'clock. And I could hear the ticket machine just going absolutely crazy back in the kitchen, just printing and printing. \n\nI could see my manager, Greg, walking toward me, and I even remember he was wearing his blue striped tie, and I could see the exact look on his face, this really specific mix of annoyed and confused. I was totally there. I could feel the cold metal of the cash drawer key digging right into the palm of my hand because I was squeezing it so hard. I just... I couldn't make my legs walk back to the register. I knew in my head that I needed to go over there, I kept telling myself to just walk to the screen and swipe my card and close out the tables, but instead I just stood there. I was smelling the fried calamari coming from the expo window, and listening to the dining room chatter, and the table numbers just kept spilling out of my mouth. \n\nSo, no, definitely no lost time. I almost wish I had blacked out, because being fully conscious while you are just stuck like that, with everyone staring at you... it is really humiliating. I remember every single detail, unfortunately.\n\nDoctor: How long did that episode by the soda station last, from when you first froze until you felt back to normal?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing about time when something like that happens, it gets really tricky to pin down exactly. Because when you're just standing there, and the ice machine is grinding, and the ticket printer is going off, seconds can feel like absolutely forever. I mean, if I had to guess, maybe it was just a few minutes? But it felt like I was standing there for an hour, just completely locked in place. \n\nI remember Greg, my manager, he came over and he was talking to me, and usually he's very quick, very impatient, you know how restaurant managers are, they always have a million fires to put out. So the fact that he actually stopped and stood there with me means it must have been long enough for him to realize something was really wrong, not just me taking a quick breather. And then he kind of guided me to the back office, which is right past the walk-in cooler, and I sat in his chair—it's this really squeaky black leather chair—and I just sat there for I don't even know how long before I finally felt like I could actually move my arms and legs properly again. \n\nSo, I don't know exactly how long the whole thing lasted from start to finish. I really couldn't give you an exact number of minutes. I just know it was long enough to completely ruin the dinner service and make everyone think I was losing my mind, which, honestly, is how it felt to me, too. It's just so hard to judge time when you're trapped in your own body like that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Aside from these freezing episodes, have you noticed anything unusual lately, like hearing voices or sounds that others don't hear, or having thoughts or beliefs that others tell you are not true?\n\nPatient: Um... well. I guess... yeah. Actually, yes. And I honestly wasn't sure if I should even mention this because it just sounds so, I don't know, ridiculous when you say it out loud, but since you're asking... yes. \n\nIt's mostly when I'm doing the closing shift. I close a lot on Tuesdays and Thursdays because I really need the extra hours, and by the end of the night, the dining room is totally empty, all the chairs are flipped upside down on the tables, and the music is off. It's usually just me out front rolling the last of the silverware or doing the final count on my drawer. You have to make sure all your cash and credit card slips match up perfectly before you can leave, which takes a lot of focus, especially after an eight-hour shift on your feet. And while I'm standing there counting, I will hear... well, I'll hear this woman's voice. \n\nAnd it's not like a faint whisper or something you could mistake for the wind outside or the hum of the big refrigerators in the back. It is loud and clear, like she is standing right over my shoulder. Sometimes she just says my name, really clearly, or... or she'll actually tell me I'm doing the count wrong. She'll say something like, 'you miscounted that,' or 'that's not the right total.' And it sounds so incredibly real, so completely normal, that my first instinct is just to respond like I would to anyone else. So I'll literally say out loud, 'No, I didn't,' or 'What are you talking about?' And then I turn around, fully expecting to see one of the other servers who maybe forgot something, or my manager, and there is absolutely no one there. The room is completely empty. \n\nAnd the weirdest part is, it's not just at the restaurant. It happens when I'm at home, too, usually when I'm in my own kitchen. I'll just be standing by the sink, maybe making some tea or wiping down the counters—I'm\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"\n\nPatient: I'm sorry, what were you asking? I just realized I completely trailed off there. I was talking about the kitchen and the tea, and I think I just lost my train of thought. Did you have another question? I tend to just keep talking once I start, especially about all of this because it's just been so overwhelming, you know? It's like my brain is just constantly running on a treadmill and I can't find the stop button. But anyway, what was it you were asking me just now?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F20.9", "F06.1", "G40.9", "F25.9"], "note": "The clear, second-person auditory hallucinations (commenting on her actions, calling her name) with preserved consciousness during the freezing episodes strengthen suspicion for a primary psychotic disorder (F20.9) or psychotic/catatonic features secondary to a mood or medical condition, while retained awareness argues against typical complex partial seizures but does not fully exclude focal seizure activity (G40.9). ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't really felt anything like that. I mean, sure, I get tired, especially after working a double shift on my feet all day, carrying those heavy trays. You know, they make us carry these massive oval trays with like six plates on them, and my shoulder just aches by the end of the night. So I'm definitely exhausted sometimes, but not depressed or hopeless. And I certainly haven't had any of those periods where I have all this extra energy or don't need sleep. If anything, I wish I had more energy because rolling silverware and dealing with customers takes so much out of you. 

But no, my mood has been pretty normal, I think. Just the usual stress of trying to make rent and hoping I get enough good tips on the weekend shifts. The weekends are always the busiest, especially Sunday brunch, which I absolutely dread because people are so particular about their eggs, but you need the money, so you do it. But as far as feeling really down or really high, no, nothing like that at all. Just the... well, just the freezing up and the voices, like I was saying before. That's really the only thing that's been out of the ordinary for me.

No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I wasn't stiff like a board or anything. If you tried to move my arm, it would just move normally, I think. No, I haven't noticed anything like that. No weird smells or anything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no head injuries. I mean, I bump my head sometimes at work because the dry storage room is this tiny little closet they shoved under the stairs, and the ceiling slants down, so if you're rushing to grab a massive box of industrial-sized ketchup packets or the extra rolls of receipt paper for the POS system, you can definitely whack your head. I did that a couple of months ago, actually, and it left a pretty nasty bump right on my hairline. It stung like crazy for a few days, but it wasn't a real injury, you know? I didn't black out or get a concussion or have to go to the hospital or anything. Dave wouldn't let me leave anyway unless I was bleeding everywhere, to be honest. But no, no actual head injuries. 

And seizures, definitely not. I've never had a seizure in my life, thank goodness. 

As for fevers or being sick... no, not really. I mean, I can't really afford to get sick. If you call out sick at the restaurant, you have to find your own cover, and nobody ever wants to pick up a Tuesday morning shift because the tips are garbage, so you just end up working through the sniffles if you have to. But I haven't had a real fever or a bad illness or anything like that recently. Just the usual seasonal allergies when the pollen gets bad, where your eyes water a little, but nothing major. I haven't had the flu or anything like that in years. 

And drugs or alcohol? No, absolutely not. I mean, when I was in my early twenties and taking some classes at the community college, maybe I'd go out for drinks after a shift with the other servers, you know, just to blow off steam because the service industry is so stressful and you just want to complain about your tables. But honestly, by the time I finish a shift now, my feet are throbbing so badly that all I want to do is go home, take my shoes off, and just sit in silence. I don't have the energy or the money for drugs or drinking. Plus, alcohol just makes me sleepy anyway, and lately, with the voices and the freezing up... well, I feel like I need to be as alert as possible just to get through the day without getting fired. So, no, I haven't taken anything or been sick or hit my head. It's just all happening completely on its own, which is why it's so frustrating.

Well, let's see... the freezing up and the voices. I'd say the really clear voices, you know, the ones that are so loud and distinct that I can't just ignore them or pretend it's the hum of the refrigerator in the kitchen... and the things I've been realizing about my coworkers and the customers, how they're all kind of in on it and targeting me—that's all been going on for about four months now.

And I just started noticing that things weren't right. The whispers started getting louder, and it became so obvious that the people at table six weren't just complaining about their food taking too long, they were actually saying things about me, specifically. And the other servers, they'd gather by the coffee station, and I knew—I just knew—they were talking about me, planning things. 

So yeah, it's been about four months of that, and it hasn't really stopped. It's been continuous. Every single shift, every single day. I wake up, and it's there. I go to work, and it's there. Even when I'm just trying to roll silverware at the end of the night, when the dining room is mostly empty and they've turned the music down, I can still hear them, and I still know what they're trying to do. It's exhausting, really, being on high alert all the time for four solid months. And the freezing episodes, those kind of just started happening alongside all of it, like my brain just gets so overwhelmed by everything they're saying and doing that it just shuts down for a minute. But yeah, four months of this, non-stop.

She'll say something like, 'you miscounted that,' or 'that's not the right total.' I just stop whatever I'm doing, and it's like my whole body just hits pause, but I'm not rigid or doing anything strange with my hands or my mouth. My stomach definitely does flips then, just from the stress of knowing I'm going to be in the weeds for the next two hours. So yeah, no warning signs or weird feelings first. It just kind of takes over out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent head injuries, seizures, fevers, or illnesses, or used any drugs or alcohol, around the time these freezing episodes and voices started?\n\nPatient: No, no head injuries. I haven't had the flu or anything like that in years. \n\nAnd drugs or alcohol? No, absolutely not. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, no, I didn't black out at all. That is actually the most frustrating part of the whole thing, to be perfectly honest with you. If I had just fainted or lost time, maybe it would make more sense to everybody, but I was completely awake and aware of every single second. I remember exactly what was happening. I remember the exact sound the ice machine was making—it was doing that loud grinding noise it does when it is refilling the bin, which it always does right around eight o'clock. And I could hear the ticket machine just going absolutely crazy back in the kitchen, just printing and printing. \n\nI could see my manager, Greg, walking toward me, and I even remember he was wearing his blue striped tie, and I could see the exact look on his face, this really specific mix of annoyed and confused. I was totally there. I could feel the cold metal of the cash drawer key digging right into the palm of my hand because I was squeezing it so hard. I just... I couldn't make my legs walk back to the register. I knew in my head that I needed to go over there, I kept telling myself to just walk to the screen and swipe my card and close out the tables, but instead I just stood there. I was smelling the fried calamari coming from the expo window, and listening to the dining room chatter, and the table numbers just kept spilling out of my mouth. \n\nSo, no, definitely no lost time. I almost wish I had blacked out, because being fully conscious while you are just stuck like that, with everyone staring at you... it is really humiliating. I remember every single detail, unfortunately.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, see, that's the thing about time when something like that happens, it gets really tricky to pin down exactly. Because when you're just standing there, and the ice machine is grinding, and the ticket printer is going off, seconds can feel like absolutely forever. I mean, if I had to guess, maybe it was just a few minutes? But it felt like I was standing there for an hour, just completely locked in place. \n\nI remember Greg, my manager, he came over and he was talking to me, and usually he's very quick, very impatient, you know how restaurant managers are, they always have a million fires to put out. So the fact that he actually stopped and stood there with me means it must have been long enough for him to realize something was really wrong, not just me taking a quick breather. And then he kind of guided me to the back office, which is right past the walk-in cooler, and I sat in his chair—it's this really squeaky black leather chair—and I just sat there for I don't even know how long before I finally felt like I could actually move my arms and legs properly again. \n\nSo, I don't know exactly how long the whole thing lasted from start to finish. I really couldn't give you an exact number of minutes. I just know it was long enough to completely ruin the dinner service and make everyone think I was losing my mind, which, honestly, is how it felt to me, too. It's just so hard to judge time when you're trapped in your own body like that, you know?\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

I close a lot on Tuesdays and Thursdays because I really need the extra hours, and by the end of the night, the dining room is totally empty, all the chairs are flipped upside down on the tables, and the music is off. It's usually just me out front rolling the last of the silverware or doing the final count on my drawer. You have to make sure all your cash and credit card slips match up perfectly before you can leave, which takes a lot of focus, especially after an eight-hour shift on your feet. And while I'm standing there counting, I will hear... well, I'll hear this woman's voice. \n\nAnd it's not like a faint whisper or something you could mistake for the wind outside or the hum of the big refrigerators in the back. It is loud and clear, like she is standing right over my shoulder. Sometimes she just says my name, really clearly, or... or she'll actually tell me I'm doing the count wrong. She'll say something like, 'you miscounted that,' or 'that's not the right total.' And it sounds so incredibly real, so completely normal, that my first instinct is just to respond like I would to anyone else. So I'll literally say out loud, 'No, I didn't,' or 'What are you talking about?' And then I turn around, fully expecting to see one of the other servers who maybe forgot something, or my manager, and there is absolutely no one there. The room is completely empty. \n\nAnd the weirdest part is, it's not just at the restaurant. It happens when I'm at home, too, usually when I'm in my own kitchen. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.   }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, no head injuries. I mean, I bump my head sometimes at work because the dry storage room is this tiny little closet they shoved under the stairs, and the ceiling slants down, so if you're rushing to grab a massive box of industrial-sized ketchup packets or the extra rolls of receipt paper for the POS system, you can definitely whack your head. I did that a couple of months ago, actually, and it left a pretty nasty bump right on my hairline. It stung like crazy for a few days, but it wasn't a real injury, you know? I didn't black out or get a concussion or have to go to the hospital or anything. Dave wouldn't let me leave anyway unless I was bleeding everywhere, to be honest. But no, no actual head injuries. \n\nAnd seizures, definitely not. I've never had a seizure in my life, thank goodness. \n\nAs for fevers or being sick... no, not really. I mean, I can't really afford to get sick. If you call out sick at the restaurant, you have to find your own cover, and nobody ever wants to pick up a Tuesday morning shift because the tips are garbage, so you just end up working through the sniffles if you have to. But I haven't had a real fever or a bad illness or anything like that recently. Just the usual seasonal allergies when the pollen gets bad, where your eyes water a little, but nothing major. I haven't had the flu or anything like that in years. \n\nAnd drugs or alcohol? No, absolutely not. I mean, when I was in my early twenties and taking some classes at the community college, maybe I'd go out for drinks after a shift with the other servers, you know, just to blow off steam because the service industry is so stressful and you just want to complain about your tables. But honestly, by the time I finish a shift now, my feet are throbbing so badly that all I want to do is go home, take my shoes off, and just sit in silence. I don't have the energy or the money for drugs or drinking. Plus, alcohol just makes me sleepy anyway, and lately, with the voices and the freezing up... well, I feel like I need to be as alert as possible just to get through the day without getting fired. So, no, I haven't taken anything or been sick or hit my head. It's just all happening completely on its own, which is why it's so frustrating.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
